                        2011 AL PYTHON MCQ MARKING

   1. Which of the following Python program segment is syntactically correct?
      my; oelafjk l=uk mhs;ka (Python) l%uf,aL LKavh ldrl kS;s wkqj ksjerÈ (syntactically correct)
      fõ o?

          1) total = 0              2) total                                                                             3) total = 0
             for i in range(1, 12):    for i in range(1, 12):                                                               for i in range(1, 12):
             total = total + i         total = total + i                                                                       total = total + i

          4) total = 0                                         5) total = 0
             for i in range(1, 12)                                   for i in range(1, 12):
                total = total + i                                       total = total + i

                                                                                                                                                         ( 2011 – 20 )

          Answer: 3)

          Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
          The correct answer is (3) because it follows all Python syntax rules.
          ksjerÈ ms<s;=r (3) jkafka th ish¨u Python ldrl kS;s wkq.ukh lrk neúks'

          total = 0
          for i in range(1, 12):
             total = total + i

          This Python code segment is syntactically correct. It initializes total to 0, then uses a for loop from 1 to 11
          (inclusive of 1, exclusive of 12) and adds each value of i to total. The indentation is proper, the colon (:)
          is present after the for loop, and the variables are valid. Therefore, option (3) is the correct and clean
          version among the choices.
          fuu mhs;ka fla; fldgi ldrl Í;S j,g wkQj ksjerÈ fõ' th uq¿ w.h 0 ka wdrïN lrhs" miqj
          1 isg 11 olajd for loop Ndú;d lrhs ^1 we;=¿j" 12 yer& iy i ys iEu w.hlau uq¿ w.hg tl;=
          lrhs' bkafvkafÜIka wjYH fõ" fld,kh (:) for loop hg miqj mj;sk w;r úp,Hhka j,x.= fõ'
          tneúka" úl,amh (3) hkq f;aÍï w;r ksjerÈ fõ'

          This syntax is accepted in both Python 2 and Python 3.
          fuu jdlH LKavh Python 2 iy Python 3 hk foflysu ms<s.kq ,efí'

         Therefore, option 3) is the correct answer.
         tneúka" 3) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 2. Consider the given flow chart:
    § we;s .e,Sï igyk i,lkak'
    Which of the following statements about the algorithm represented by the flow chart is incorrect?
    .e,Sï igyk u.ska ksrEmKh flfrk we,af.dß;uh ms<sn|j my; oelafjk ljr m%ldYh idjoH
    fõo?
                              START

                             Let X = 5

                       I have a positive integer.
                           Can you guess it?

                           Get a number Y
                            from the user

                     Yes
          STOP               Is Y < 0 ?
                            No
        You are      Yes
                             Is X = Y ?
        correct.
                             No

                                  Is
                                Y–X           Yes    You are
                            less than 3 ?           very close.

                                   No

    1) It stops when the user enters the value –1 / mßYS,lhd –1 w.h we;=¿ l< úg th kj;shs'
    2) It stops when the user enters the value 5 / mßYS,lhd 5 w.h we;=¿ l< úg th kj;shs'
    3) It gives the message “You are very close.” when the user enters the value 9.
       mßYS,lhd 9 w.h we;=¿ l< úg th" “Tn b;d iómhs'” hk mKsúvh fohs'
    4) It gives the message “You are very close.” when the user enters the value 6.
       mßYS,lhd 6 w.h we;=¿ l< úg th" “Tn b;d iómhs'” hk mKsúvh fohs'
    5) If the value entered by the user is 8, it allows the user to enter another value.
       mßYS,lhd we;=¿ l< w.h 8 kï" Tyqg fjkka eeeeew.hla we;=¿ lsÍug th bvfohs'

                                                                                                      ( 2011 – 21 )
    Answer: 3)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    The flowchart gets a user input Y, and checks if it is negative. If not, it checks whether Y == X (where X
    = 5) or if the absolute difference |Y - X| < 3. If Y == X, it prints "You are correct", and if Y is no more than
    3 units away from X, it prints "You are very close".
    .e,Sï igykg mßYS,l wdodkh Y ,efnk w;r th iDK oehs mÍlaId lrhs' tfia fkdfõ kï" th
    Y == X (fuys§ X = 5) fyda ksrfmalaI fjki |Y - X| < 3 mÍlaId lrhs' Y == X kï" th "Tn ksjerÈhs""
    iy Y ys w.h X g jvd tAll 3 lg jvd ÿßka fkdfõ kï" th "Tn b;d iómhs" hehs uqøKh lrhs'

    So when Y = 9, Y - X = 4, which is not less than 3. Therefore, statement (3) - “It gives the message 'You
    are very close.' when the user enters 9” - is incorrect, and that makes it the correct answer to this question.
    tneúka Y = 9 jk úg" Y - X = 4" th 3 g fkdwvq fõ' tneúka" (3) - “mßYS,lhd 9 we;=<;a l< úg
    th 'Tn b;d iómhs' hk mKSúvh ,nd fohs” - fuu m%ldYh jerÈ jk w;r" th fuu m%Yakhg
    ksjerÈ ms<s;=r njg m;a lrhs'

 3. Consider the following Python data items:
    my; oelafjk mhs;ka (Python) o;a; whs;u i,ld n,kak'

    A. 15.2
    B. [12, 'abc', 5.2]
    C. {'name' : 'Nimal', 'age' : 18}

    Python data types of the above data items A, B, C are respectively
    by; A, B, C o;a; whs;uj, Python o;a; m%rEm (Data types) ms<sfj<ska

    1)   float, list, dictionary.
    2)   integer, tuple, dictionary.
    3)   float, list, tuple.
    4)   integer, tuple, list.
    5)   float, tuple, dictionary.
                                                                                                    ( 2011 – 37 )

    Answer: 1)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    The correct answer is (1) float, list, dictionary. The value A = 15.2 includes a decimal point, which means
    it is a float type in Python. The value B = [12, 'abc', 5.2] is enclosed in square brackets, making it a list.
    Lists in Python can store multiple data types, including integers, strings, and floats.
    ksjerÈ ms<s;=r jkafka (1) float, list, dictionary fõ' A = 15.2 w.hg oYu ,laIHhla we;=<;a fõ" tkï
    th Python ys oYuh (float) j¾.hls' B = [12, 'abc', 5.2] w.h y;/ia jryka ;=< oud we;s w;r
    th ,ehsia;=jla (list) njg m;a lrhs' Python ys ,ehsia;= j,g mQ¾K ixLHd" strings iy floats we;=¿
    nyq o;a; j¾. .nvd l< yelsh'

    The value C = {'name': 'Nimal', 'age': 18} is a group of key-value pairs enclosed in curly brackets, which
    defines a dictionary. This structure is used to store related data with keys and values. The data types for A,
    B and C remain the same in both Python 2 and Python 3.
    C = {'name': 'Nimal', 'age': 18} w.h hkq dictionary hla w¾: olajk curly jryka ;=< oud we;s
    h;=re-w.h hq., iuQyhls' fuu jHqyh h;=re iy w.hka iuÕ wod< o;a; .nvd lsÍug Ndú;d
    lrhs' A, B iy C i|yd o;a; j¾. Python 2 iy Python 3 hk foflysu tf,iu mj;S'

    Therefore, option 1) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 1) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 4. Consider the following statements about programming languages:
    l%uf,aL NdId ms<sn|j my; oelafjk m%ldY i,ld n,kak'

    A. C programming language is a first-generation language (1GL).
       C l%uf,aL NdIdj" m<uqjk mrïmrdfõ (1GL) NdIdjls'
    B. Assembly language is a second-generation language (2GL).
       Assembly NdIdj fojk mrïmrdfõ (2GL) NdIdjls'
    C. Python is a second-generation language (2GL)
       Python fojk mrïmrdfõ (2GL) NdIdjls'

    Which of the above statements is/are correct?
    by; m%ldY w;=frka i;H jkafka l=ula o?

    1) A only.         2) B only.          3) C only.          4) A and B only.      5) B and C only.
       A muKs.            B muKs.             C muKs.             A yd B muKs.          B yd C muKs.

                                                                                                   (2011 - 45)

    Answer: 2)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Statement / m%ldYh A)
    Statement A is incorrect because C is not a first-generation language (1GL); it is a third-generation
    language (3GL), designed to be more human-readable and portable. First-generation languages are
    machine-level languages, consisting only of binary code (0s and 1s).
    C hkq m<uq mrïmrdfõ NdIdjla fkdjk ksid A m%ldYh jerÈhs (1GL); th ;=kajk mrïmrdfõ
    NdIdjla (3GL)" ñksiqkag jvd;a lshúh yels iy wf;a f.k hd yels jk mßÈ ks¾udKh lr we;'
    m<uq mrïmrdfõ NdId hkq oaúuh fla; ^0s iy 1s& j,ska muKla iukaú; hka;% uÜgfï NdId fõ'

    Statement / m%ldYh B)
    Statement B is correct because assembly language is considered a second-generation language (2GL). It
    uses symbolic instructions (like MOV, ADD, etc.) that are directly translated into machine code using an
    assembler.
    Assembly NdIdj fojk mrïmrdfõ NdIdjla (2GL) f,i i,lkq ,nk ksid B m%ldYh ksjerÈhs' th
    Assembler flfkl= Ndú;fhka hka;% fla;hg fl,skau mßj¾;kh lrk ixfla;d;aul Wmfoia ^ MOV,
    ADD wdÈh& Ndú;d lrhs'

    Statement / m%ldYh C)
    Statement C is incorrect because Python is a high-level language, which places it in the category of third-
    generation languages (3GL), not second-generation. This classification remains the same in both Python
    2 and Python 3.
    C m%ldYh jerÈ fõ" ukao Python hkq by< uÜgfï NdIdjla jk w;r" th fojk mrïmrdfõ fkdj
    ;=kajk mrïmrdfõ NdId (3GL) ldKavhg we;=<;a lrhs' fuu j¾.SlrKh Python 2 iy Python 3
    hk foflysu tf,iu mj;S'

    Therefore, the correct answer is 2) B only.
    tneúka" ksjerÈ ms<s;=r 2) B muKs'

 5. Consider the following statements in a program:
    l%uf,aLhlg wh;a my; oelafjk m%ldY i,ld n,kak'

    A. # This is a comment.
    B. // This is a comment.
    C. /* This is a comment. */
    D. a=1 # This is a comment.
    E. # Initial value of a=1

    Which of the above are syntactically correct Python statements?
    by; oelafjk tajd w;=frka ldrl Í;sj,g wkql+,j (Syntactically) ksjerÈ mhs;ka (Python) m%ldY
    jkafka fudkjd o?

    1) A and D only. 2) C and E only.       3) A, D and E only. 4) B, C and D only. 5) C, D and E only.
       A yd D muKs.     C yd E muKs.           A, D yd E muKs.     B, C yd D muKs.     C, D yd E muKs.

                                                                                                  ( 2011 – 46 )

    Answer: 3)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Statement A is syntactically correct in Python because comments begin with the # symbol. Statement D is
    also valid - it assigns 1 to the variable a, and everything after the # is treated as a comment. Statement E
    is another valid standalone comment beginning with #, which Python recognizes and ignores during
    execution.
    m%ldYh A mhs;ka ys ldrl Í;s j,g wkQj ksjerÈ jkafka újrKh # ixfla;fhka wdrïN jk neúks'
    m%ldYh D o j,x.= fõ - th a úp,Hhg 1 mjrk w;r # g miqj we;s ish,a, újrKhla f,i
    i,lkq ,efí' m%ldYh E hkq # iuÕ wdrïN jk ;j;a j,x.= iajdëk újrKhla jk w;r th
    l%shd;aul lsÍfï§ mhs;ka y÷kdf.k fkdi,ld yßhs'

    Statement B (//) and C (/* */) are not valid comment styles in Python. Those styles are used in other
    languages like Java, C or C++, but not in Python. In both Python 2 and Python 3, only # is used for single-
    line comments. Therefore, only statements A, D and E are syntactically correct.
    m%ldYh B (//) iy C (/* */) Python ys j,x.= újrK ú,di fkdfõ' tu ú,dihka Java, C fyda C++
    jeks wfkl=;a NdIdj,ska Ndú;d fõ" kuq;a Python ys fkdfõ' Python 2 iy Python 3 hk foflysu"
    ;ks fma<s woyia i|yd # muKla Ndú;d fõ' tneúka" A, D iy E m%ldY muKla ldrl Í;S j,g
    wkQj ksjerÈ fõ'

    Therefore, the correct answer is 3) A, D and E only.
    tneúka" ksjerÈ ms<s;=r 3) A, D iy E muKS fõ'

 6. Consider the following assignment statements:
    my; oelafjk mejreï m%ldY i,lkak'

    A. a = 'Nimal's address'
    B. a = "Nimal's address"
    C. a, b, c = l
    D. a, b, c = 1, 2, 'string'
    E. a = b = 1

    Which of the above are syntactically correct Python statements?
    by; oelafjk tajd w;=frka ldrl Í;sj,g wkql+,j (Syntactically) ksjerÈ mhs;ka (Python) m%ldY
    jkafka fudkjd o?

    1) A and C only.   2) B and D only. 3) A, C and E only. 4) B, D and E only. 5) C, D and E only.
       A yd C muKs.       B yd D muKs.     A, C yd E muKs.     B, D yd E muKs.     C, D yd E muKs.

                                                                                                 ( 2011 – 47 )

    Answer: 4)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Statement A is wrong because the single quote inside the string isn’t handled properly, causing a syntax
    error in both Python 2 and 3.
    m%ldYh A jerÈ fõ" ukao string tl ;=< we;s ;ks Wmqgd oelaùu ksis f,i yiqrejd fkdue;s w;r"
    th Python 2 iy 3 hk foflysu jdlH LKav fodaIhla we;s lrhs'

    Statement B is correct because the string uses double quotes, so the single quote inside is fine.
    B m%ldYh ksjerÈ jkafka string tl oaú;aj Wmqgd oelaùï Ndú;d lrk neúks" we;=<; we;s ;ks Wmqgd
    oelaùu m%YaKhla fkdfõ'

    Statement C can be correct only if l is a list or tuple with exactly three items.
    m%ldYh C ksjerÈ úh yelafla 1 hkq yßhgu whs;a;ï ;=kla iys; ,ehsia;=jla fyda gm,a tlla kï
    muKS'

    Statements D and E are both correct in Python 2 and 3 - D assigns three values to three variables, and E
    assigns the same value to two variables.
    m%ldY D iy E hk folu Python 2 iy 3 ys ksjerÈ fõ - D úp,H ;=klg w.hka ;=kla mjrk
    w;r E úp,H follg tlu w.h mjrhs'

    So, the syntactically correct statements are B, D and E only. This matches option (4). Python 2 and Python
    3 behave the same way for these statements, so the answer is the same for both versions.
    tneúka" jdlH LKavuh jYfhka ksjerÈ m%ldY jkafka B, D iy E muKS' fuh úl,amh (4) g .e,fma'
    Python 2 iy Python 3 fuu m%ldY i|yd tlu wdldrhlska yeisfrk neúka" wkqjdo foflysu ms<s;=r
    iudk fõ'

    Therefore, the correct answer is 4) B, D and E only.
    tneúka" ksjerÈ ms<s;=r 4) B, D iy E muKS fõ'

 7. Consider the following incomplete Python program:
    my; oelafjk wiïmQ¾K mhs;ka (Python) l%uf,aLh i,lkak:

    data=[5, 1, 23, 10]
    datacount = len(data)
    for i in range(datacount-1):
        for k in range (i, datacount):

            temp = data[i]
            data[i],data[k] =data[k],temp
    for i in range(datacount):
            print (data[i])

    Which of the following Python statements should be included at the blank line to print the data items
    in thedata structure named ‘data’ in the ascending order.
    ‘data’ keue;s o;a; jHqyh ;=< we;s o;a; whs;u wdfrdayK mámdáhg uqøKh lsÍu i|yd ysia
    fma<sfhys wka;¾.; l< hq;af;a my; oelafjk ljr mhs;ka (Python) m%ldYh o?

    1) if data[i] < data[k]: 2) if data[i] > data[k]: 3) if data[i] = data[k]:
    4) if data[i] < data[k] 5) if data[i] > data[k]

                                                                                                   ( 2011 – 48 )

    Answer: 2)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    The code is using selection sort logic to sort the list data in ascending order. To do this, it compares the
    current item data[i] with the rest of the items data[k] from index i to the end. If a smaller item is found
    (i.e., data[k] < data[i]), it swaps the two. This means the condition must check if data[i] > data[k], and if
    so, perform the swap.
    ,ehsia;= o;a; wdfrdayK wkqms<sfj,g j¾. lsÍu i|yd fla;h f;aÍï j¾. lsÍfï ;¾lkh Ndú;d
    lrhs' fuh isÿ lsÍu i|yd" th j;auka whs;u data[i] o¾Ylh i isg wjidkh olajd b;sß whs;u
    data[k] iuÕ ixikaokh lrhs' l=vd whs;uhla yuq jqjfyd;a ^tkï" data[k] < data[i]&" th fol udre
    lrhs' fuhska woyia lrkafka fldkafoaish data[i] > data[k] oehs mÍlaId l< hq;= w;r" tfia kï"
    yqjudrej isÿ l< hq;= njhs'

    So the correct line to insert is / tneúka we;=¿ lsÍug ksjerÈ fma<sh jkafka(
    if data[i] > data[k]:

    This matches option (2). The colon at the end is important to define the if block correctly in Python.In
    both Python 2 and Python 3, the behavior of this code will be the same.
    fuh (2) úl,amhg .e,fma' Python ys if íf,dla tl ksjerÈj w¾: oelaùug wjidkfha we;s fld,kh
    jeo.;a fõ' Python 2 iy Python 3 hk foflysu" fuu fla;fha yeisÍu iudk jkq we;'

    Therefore, option 2) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 2) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 8. Consider the following Python program:
    my; oelafjk mhs;ka (Python) l%uf,aLh i,lkak'

    data = [5, 1, 23, 10, –3]
    def fun(a):
         i, c=1, a[0]
         while i < len(a):
            if(a[i] > c):
                  c=a[i]
            i=i+i
         return c
    print (fun(data))

    Which of the following is the output of this program?
    my; oelafjk tajd w;=frka fuu jevigyfkys m%;sodkh (Output) l=ula o?

    1) –3               2) 1                3) 5                4) 10               5) 23

                                                                                                   ( 2011 – 49 )

    Answer: 5)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    The program defines a function that finds the largest number from certain positions in the list data = [5, 1,
    23, 10, -3]. It starts with i = 1 and c = 5 (the first element). Inside the loop, it checks values at index
    positions that double each time (i = 1, 2, 4……). It compares each of those values with c and updates c if a
    bigger value is found. The values checked are 1, 23 and -3, and the largest among them is 23, which gets
    returned.
    fuu jevigyk uÕska ,ehsia;= data = [5, 1, 23, 10, -3] ys we;eï ia:dk j,ska úYd,;u ixLHdj fidhd
    .kakd Y%s;hla ks¾jpkh lrhs' th i = 1 iy c = 5 ^m<uq uQ,dx.h& iuÕ wdrïN fõ' Æmh ;=<" th
    iEu wjia:djlu fo.=K jk o¾Yl ia:dkj, w.hka mÍlaId lrhs (i = 1, 2, 4……)' th tu tla tla
    w.hka c iuÕ ixikaokh lr úYd, w.hla yuq jqjfyd;a c hdj;ald,Sk lrhs' mÍlaId lrk ,o
    w.hka 1, 23 iy -3 jk w;r" tAjd w;r úYd,;u w.h 23 jk w;r th wdmiq ,nd fokq ,efí'

    This program works the same in both Python 2 and Python 3.
    fuu jevigyk Python 2 iy Python 3 hk foflysu tlu wdldrhlska l%shd lrhs'

    Therefore, option 5) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 5) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

  9. Consider the following Python program:
     my; oelafjk mhs;ka (Python) l%uf,aLh i,lkak'

     f1 = open('input.txt','r')
     f2 = open('output.txt', 'w')
     line = f1.readline()
     while (line) :
            data = (line.strip()).split(",")
            total = float(data[1]) + float(data[2])
            f2.write('{},{},{},{}\n'.format(data[0],data[1],data[2],total))
             line = f1.readline()
     f1.close()
     f2.close()

     The content of the file “input.txt” is given below.
     “input.txt” f.dkqfjys wka;¾.;h my; § we;.
     Nimal,30,60
     Saman,80,45
     Upali,100,80

     After executing the program what would be the content of the file “output.txt”?
     jevigyk l%shd;aul lsÍfuka miqj “output.txt” f.dkqfjys wka;¾.;h l=ula fõ o?

     1) Nimal                        2) Nimal,30,60                      3) Nimal,30,60,90
        Saman                           Saman,80,45                         Saman,80,45,125
        Upali                           Upali,100,80                        Upali,100,80,180
     4) Nimal,30,60,90.0             5) Nimal,30,60,90.0 Saman,80,45,125.0 Upali,100,80,180.0
        Saman,80,45,125.0
        Upali,100,80,180.0

     Answer: 4)

     Nimal,30,60,90.0

                       2012 AL PYTHON MCQ MARKING

   1. What is the result of evaluating the Python expression 10 % 3?
      10%3 hk mhs;ka (Python) m%ldYkh we.hSfuys m%;sM,h l=ula o?

          1) 0                          2) 1                          3) 2                          4) 3                          5) 10

                                                                                                                                                         ( 2012 – 19 )
          Answer: 2)

          Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
          The correct answer is (2) 1. In Python, the % operator is the modulus operator, which returns the remainder
          after dividing the first number by the second. So, 10 % 3 means divide 10 by 3 and return the remainder.
          ksjerÈ ms<s;=r jkafka (2) 1 fõ' mhs;ka ys" % l%shdlre hkq fudähq,ia l%shdlre jk w;r" th m<uq
          wxlh fojekafkka fn§fuka miq b;sßh wdmiq ,nd fohs' tneúka" 10 % 3 hkafkka woyia lrkafka 10
          ÷ 3fkka fn¥ úg b;sßh wdmiq ,nd §uhs'

          When 10 is divided by 3, the quotient is 3 and the remainder is 1, because 3 × 3 = 9 and 10 - 9 = 1. So, 10
          % 3 evaluates to 1, and this behavior is the same in both Python 2 and Python 3.
          10 ÷ 3fkka fn¥ úg" m%;sY;h 3 jk w;r b;sßh 1 fõ" ukao 3 × 3 = 9 iy 10 - 9 = 1' tneúka" 10
          % 3 1g we.hSug ,la lrk w;r" fuu yeisÍu mhs;ka 2 iy mhs;ka 3 hk foflysu iudk fõ'

         Therefore, option 2) is the correct answer.
         tneúka" 2) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

   2. Consider the following pseudo code:
      my; olafjk jHdc fla;h (pseudo code) i,lkak(

          Begin
              total = 0
              For count = 1 To 10
                  If (count is odd) Then
                        total = total + count
                  EndIf
              Next count
              Display total
          End

          What is the output of the above pseudo code?
          by; jHdc fla;fhys m%;sodkh (output) l=ula o?

          1) 10                         2) 15                         3) 20                         4) 25                         5) 55

          Answer: 4)

     Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
     The correct answer is (4) 25. The pseudo code initializes `total = 0` and loops `count` from 1 to 10. For
     each value of `count`, it checks if the number is odd, and if so, adds it to `total`. The odd numbers between
     1 and 10 are: 1, 3, 5, 7, 9.
     ksjerÈ ms<s;=r jkafka (4) 25' jHdc fla;h total = 0 wdrïN lrk w;r count 1 isg 10 olajd Æma

 5. Consider the following assignment statements:
    my; olafjk mejreï m%ldY i,ld n,kak(

    A - a, b = "Nimal", 30                      B - a, b = 1                        C - a=b=1
    D - a = True                                E - a = true

    Which of the above are syntactically correct Python statements?
    jd.aÍ;Sj,g wkql+,j ksjerÈ jkafka by; oelafjk ljr mhs;ka (Python) m%ldY o?

    1) A and C only.     2) B and D only. 3) A, C and D only. 4) B, D and E only. 5) C, D and E only.
       A yd C muKs.         B yd D muKs.     A, C yd D muKs.     B, D yd E muKs.     C, D yd E muKs.

                                                                                                        ( 2012 – 45 )

    Answer: 3)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    Statement / m%ldYh A)
    (a, b = "Nimal", 30) is syntactically correct because it uses tuple unpacking to assign "Nimal" to `a` and
    `30` to `b`.
    (a, b = "Nimal", 30) ldrl Í;Suhj ksjerÈ jkafka th "Nimal" `a` g iy `30` `b` g mejÍug tuple
    unpacking Ndú;d lrk neúks'

    Statement / m%ldYh C)
    (a = b = 1) is also valid, assigning the value `1` to both `b` and `a` in a chained assignment.
    (a = b = 1) o j,x.= jk w;r" odu iys; mejreul `b` iy `a` hk folgu `1` w.h mejÍu isÿ
    lrhs'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                 -13-                                            #ictfromabc

    Statement / m%ldYh D)
    (a = True) is valid as well - `True` is a boolean constant in Python, and assigning it to a variable is allowed.
    (a = True) o j,x.= fõ - `True` hkq Python ys nQ,shka ksh;hla jk w;r th úp,Hhlg mejÍug
    wjir we;'

    Statement B (a, b = 1) is incorrect because the right-hand side provides only one value, while the left
    expects two, which causes a ValueError. Statement E (`a = true`) is invalid because Python is case-sensitive
    and `true` (lowercase) is undefined — the correct boolean value is `True` with a capital T. This syntax
    validation holds the same in both Python 2 and Python 3.
    m%ldYh B (a, b = 1) jerÈ fõ" ukao ol=Kq mi tla w.hla muKla imhk w;r jï miska folla
    wfmalaId lrk w;r th ValueError we;s lrhs' Python wlaIr-ixfõ§ jk w;r `true` ^l=vd wl=re&
    ks¾jpkh lr fkdue;s neúka E (`a = true`) m%ldYh j,x.= fkdfõ - ksjerÈ nQ,shka w.h T wl=ßka
    `True` fõ' fuu jdlH LKav j,x.=lrKh Python 2 iy Python 3 hk foflysu tlu wdldrhlska
    mj;S'

    Therefore, the correct answer is 3) A, C and D only.
    tneúka" ksjerÈ ms<s;=r 3) A, C iy D muKs'

 6. Consider the following statements about Python data types:
    mhs;ka o;a; m%rEm ms<sn| my; olafjk m%ldY i,lkak(

       tuple m%rEmfhys o;a;hla fldudj,ska fjka lrkq ,nk mámdá.; w.hkaf.ka iukaú; fõ'

    Which of the above statements is/are correct?
    by; m%ldY w;=frka i;H jkafka ljrla$ljr tajd o?

       A muKs.             B muKs.              C muKs.              A yd B muKs.           B yd C muKs.

    Answer: 4)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    Statement / m%ldYh A)
    Statement A is correct because a tuple in Python is a collection of ordered values, separated by commas,
    and usually enclosed in parentheses. The order of elements is preserved, and each element can be accessed
    using its index.
    Python ys tuple tlla hkq fldud j,ska fjka lrk ,o iy idudkHfhka jryka ;=< fldgd we;s
    wkqms<sfj,g ilid we;s w.hka tl;=jla jk neúka A m%ldYh ksjerÈ fõ' uQ,dx.j, wkqms<sfj,
    ixrlaIKh lr we;s w;r" iEu uQ,dx.hlau tys o¾Ylh Ndú;fhka m%fõY úh yelsh'

    Statement / m%ldYh B)
    Statement B is also correct. A dictionary in Python is an unordered collection of `key: value` pairs,
    separated by commas and enclosed within curly braces `{}`. Each key must be unique, and values can be
    of any data type.
    B m%ldYh o ksjerÈ fõ' Python ys dictionary hla hkq fldud j,ska fjka lr we;s iy curly braces
    `{}` ;=< fldgd we;s `key : value` hq.,j, wkqms<sfj,g ilia fkdl< tl;=jls' iEu h;=rlau woaú;Sh
    úh hq;= w;r" w.hka ´kEu o;a; j¾.hl úh yelsh'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                               -14-                                         #ictfromabc

    Statement / m%ldYh C)
    Statement C is incorrect because tuples are immutable, meaning once created, their values cannot be
    changed, unlike dictionaries which are mutable. This behavior is the same in both Python 2 and Python 3.
    C m%ldYh jerÈ fõ" ukao tuples fjkia l< fkdyels neúka" tkï ks¾udKh l< miq" tAjdfha w.hka
    fjkia l< fkdyels dictionary fuka fkdj fjkia l< fkdyel' fuu yeisÍu Python 2 iy Python 3

 7. Which of the following Python program is syntactically correct?

       print(i,'*',j,'=',i*j)                                       print(i,'*',j,'=',i*j)
       print()                                                  print()

       #Generate the multiplication table of i                  #Generate the multiplication table of i
             print('Multiplication table of',i)                   print('Multiplication table of',i)

       #Generate the multiplication table of i
             print('Multiplication table of',i)

             print():

    Answer: 3)

    ksjerÈ ms<s;=r (3) jkafka th ish¨u Python ldrl kS;s wkq.ukh lrk neúks'

    for i in [8,9,10,11,12]:
    #Generate the multiplication table of i
          print('Multiplication table of',i)
          for j in range(1,12):
              print(i,'*',j,'=',i*j)
         print()

    The for loops include colons (:) at the end, which are mandatory to start the loop blocks. All the print()
    functions are written correctly with parentheses, and assuming proper indentation is followed (which is
    often required but not shown in plain text), this code would work as expected to generate multiplication
    tables. Comments using # are also correctly placed, and the range function is properly used.
    for loops wjidkfha colon (:) we;=<;a jk w;r" tAjd loop blocks wdrïN lsÍug wksjd¾h fõ' ish¨u
    print() Y%s; jryka iuÕ ksjerÈj ,shd we;s w;r" ksis bkafvkafÜIka wkq.ukh lr we;ehs Wml,amkh

    lsÍug wfmalaId lrk mßÈ l%shd lrhs' # i,l=K u.ska fla;h Ndú;d lrk újrK o ksjerÈj ;nd
    we;s w;r" mrdi Y%s;h ksis f,i Ndú;d fõ'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                -15-                                           #ictfromabc

    The other options have clear syntax errors. Option (2) is missing a colon after the first for loop. Option (1)
    and (5) contain formatting issues Option (5) even ends a print() line with a colon, which is invalid. Option
    (4) mixes integers and strings like '9' and '11', which leads to a TypeError when attempting multiplication
    with integers. This correct syntax in option (3) works in both Python 2 and Python 3, though print() must
    use parentheses in Python 3.
    wfkl=;a úl,amj, meyeÈ,s ldrl Í;s fodaI we;' m<uq for loop tflka miqj (2) úl,amfha colon
    tlla fkdue;' úl,amh (1) iy (5) wdlD;slrK .eg¿ wvx.= fõ úl,amh (5) print() Y%s;h mjd

    ñY% lrhs" th mQ¾K ixLHd iuÕ .=K lsÍug W;aidy lrk úg TypeError tllg u. mdohs' úl,amh
    (3) ys fuu ksjerÈ jdlH LKavh Python 2 iy Python 3 hk foflysu l%shd lrhs" kuq;a print()
    Python 3 ys jryka wksjd¾hfhkau Ndú;d l< hq;=h'

    That is, as follows. / tkï" my; mßÈ fõ'
    In Python 2, you could write a print statement like this
    Python 2 ys" fujeks uqøK m%ldYhla ,súh yelsh

    •   print "ict, fromabc"

    But in Python 3, print became a function, so you need to use parentheses
    kuq;a Python 3 ys" uqøKh Y%s;hla njg m;a úh" tneúka jryka Ndú;d l< hq;=h

    •   print("ict, fromabc")

    Therefore, option 3) is the correct answer.

 8. Consider the following incomplete Python program:
    my; oelafjk wiïmQ¾K mhs;ka l%uf,aLh i,lkak(

    name = ['Kamal', 'Ruwan', 'Nimal', 'Wimal']
    datacount = len(name)
    for i in range(datacount - 1):
      for k in range(i+1, datacount):
        if ……
              name[i],name[k] = name[k],name[i]

    To arrange the data items in the name data structure in the reverse alphabetic order, which of the
    following Python statements should be included at the blank line?
    name o;a; jHqyfhys o;a; whs;u m%;sj¾;H wldrd§ ms<sfj<g (in the reverse alphabetic order)
    ms<sfh, lsÍu i|yd by; ysia;efkys" my; oelafjk ljr mhs;ka m%ldYh we;=<;a l< hq;= o?

    1) name[i] < name[k] :             2) name[i] > name[k] :              3) name[i] = name[k] :
    4) name[i] < name[k] ;             5) name[i] > name[k] ;

                                                                                                    ( 2012 – 48 )
    Answer: 1)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    To sort the list in reverse alphabetical order, you need to swap elements when the one at index `i` is
    alphabetically less than the one at index `k`. That’s because you want the bigger (later in the alphabet)
    items to come before the smaller ones. For example, since `"Kamal" < "Ruwan"` is `True`, they should be
    swapped to put `"Ruwan"` before `"Kamal"`.
    ,ehsia;=j m%;sf,dau wldrd§ ms<sfj,g j¾. lsÍug" `i` o¾Ylfha we;s tl `k` o¾Ylfha we;s tlg
    jvd wldrd§ f,i wvq jQ úg Tn uQ,dx. udre l< hq;=h' thg fya;=j Tng úYd, ^miqj fydaäfha&
    whs;a;u l=vd tAjdg fmr meñKsug wjYH neúks' WodyrKhla f,i" `Kamal < Ruan` `True` neúka"
    tAjd `Kamal` g fmr `Ruwan` ;eîug udre l< hq;=h'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                 -16-                                            #ictfromabc

    Using `name[i] < name[k]` with a colon (`:`) ensures the correct sorting condition and proper Python
    syntax. Options (3), (4) and (5) are invalid either due to incorrect logic or incorrect punctuation (a
    semicolon instead of a colon). This logic works the same in both Python 2 and Python 3.

 9. Consider the following Python program:
    my; oelafjk mhs;ka l%uf,aLh i,lkak(
    def fun(a):
          i, c, j = 1, a[0],0
          while i < len(a):
                if (a[i] > c):
                     c = a[i]
                     j = i
                i = i + 1
          return j
    print (fun([5, 2, 23, 10, –3]))

    What is the output of this program?
    fuu l%uf,aLfhys m%;sodkh (output) l=ula o?

    1) 23               2) 10                3) 5                 4) 2                5) –3
                                                                                                      ( 2012 – 49 )
    Answer: 4)
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    The given Python program is designed to find the index of the largest number in the list a. Initially, the
    variables are set as i = 1, c = a[0] (which is 5), and j = 0. The while loop runs through the list starting from
    index 1. For each element, it checks whether the current item a[i] is greater than the current maximum c.
    If it is, it updates c with that value and records its index in j. After the loop finishes, it returns j, which
    holds the index of the highest value in the list. / § we;s mhs;ka jevigyk a ,ehsia;=fõ we;s úYd,;u
    ixLHdfõ o¾Ylh fidhd .ekSug ks¾udKh lr we;' uq,§" úp,Hhka i = 1, c = a[0] ^th 5 iy j = 0

    tu w.h iuÕ c hdj;ald,Sk lr tys o¾Ylh j ys jd¾;d lrhs' Æmh wjika jQ miq" th ,ehsia;=fõ
    by<u w.fha o¾Ylh r|jd .kakd j wdmiq ,nd fohs'

 10. Consider the following Python program:
     my; oelafjk mhs;ka l%uf,aLh i,lkak(

    f1 = open('input.txt', 'r')
    f2 = open('output.txt', 'w')
    for line in f1:
          data = (line.strip()).split(",")
          total = float(data[1]) + float(data[2])
          f2.write('%7s-%4d\n'% (data[0],total))
    f1.close()
    f2.close()

    The content of the file "input.txt" is given below.
    "input.txt" f.dkqfjys wka;¾.;h my; § we;'

    Nimal,30,60

       Saman,80,45,125
       Upali,100,80,180

    Answer: 2)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    calculates the total by adding the second and third values as floating-point numbers. The result is then
    written to output.txt using the formatting '%7s-%4d\n', where %7s ensures the name is right-aligned in a
    7-character space and %4d formats the total as an integer. This means the decimal part is removed, and
    values like 90.0 will be written as 90.

   1. What is the output of the following Python program?:

                                                                                                                                                         ( 2013 – 10 )
          Answer: 2)

          Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

          The given Python program defines a string `s` with the value "Nimal Perera". The code then prints a slice
          of the string, `s[1:3]`. This slicing operation means that the program extracts characters starting from index
          `1` up to, but not including, index `3`. Since Python uses zero-based indexing, the character at index `1`
          is `'i'`, and the character at index `2` is `'m'`. Thus, the slice `s[1:3]` results in the substring `"im"`. So, the
          correct answer is (2) im.
          § we;s mhs;ka jevigyk wkQj "Nimal Perera" hk value tl iys; `s` string tl w¾: olajhs'

          jkafka (2) im fõ'

          These theories remain consistent between Python 2 and Python 3.
          fuu kHdhka Python 2 iy Python 3 w;r fkdfjkiaj mj;S'

         Therefore, option 2) is the correct answer.

 2. Which of the following is a valid Python statement that can be used to open a file called
    "output.txt" so that new data can be added to the end of the file without deleting its original
    content?
    uq,ska wvx.= oE uld fkdouñka" kj o;a;" f.dkqfõ w.g tl;= l< yels mßoafoka "output.txt"

    5) infile = open ("output.txt", "w")

                                                                                                   ( 2013 – 11 )
    Answer: 3)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    The correct answer is (3) infile = open("output.txt", "a"). In Python, the "a" mode (append mode) is used
    to open a file so that data can be added to its end without erasing the existing content. The file pointer is
    positioned at the end, ensuring that all new data is written after the existing data. This makes "a" the most
    appropriate mode for preserving the file's content while adding new information.

    recognized in Python. Option (2) uses "r" mode, which allows reading but not writing or appending to the
    file. Option (5) uses "w" mode, which overwrites the file and erases its original content.

 5. Which of the following is an invalid Python identifier?
    my; i|yka oE w;=ßka j,x.= fkdjk mhs;ka y÷kajkh (identifier) l=ula o?

    Answer: 4)
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    The correct answer is (4) 6Name. In Python, identifiers (names for variables, functions, etc.) must begin
    with a letter (A-Z or a-z) or an underscore (`_`). They cannot start with a digit. While `_name`, `Name`,
    `Name_` and `_6_names` follow these rules, `6Name` starts with the digit `6`, making it invalid.

    iuÕ wdrïN jk w;r th j,x.= fkdlrhs'

    Rules for Naming Variables in Python / mhs;ka ys úp,Hhka kï lsÍu i|yd jk kS;s'

     Rule                                                    Example                   Valid?
     Must start with a letter or underscore                  _var, name                Yes
     wl=rlska fyda há brlska wdrïN l< hq;=h
     Cannot start with a digit                               6name                     No
     b,lalulska wdrïN l< fkdyel
     Can contain letters, digits, or underscores             var_123                   Yes
     wl=re" b,lalï fyda há bß wvx.= úh yelsh
     Cannot use Python keywords                              class, if                 No
     mhs;ka uQ, mo Ndú;d l< fkdyel
     Case-sensitive                                          name, Name                Yes
     wlaIr ixfõ§ fõ

    This rule applies consistently in both Python 2 and Python 3.
    fuu Í;sh Python 2 iy Python 3 hk foflysu wLKavj wod< fõ'

    resulting in [1, 2, 3, 4]. The `print(c)` statement then outputs this concatenated list.

    `+` l%shdlre Ndú;d lrñka ,ehsia;= tAldnoaO lsÍfï fuu yeisÍu Python 2 iy Python 3 hk foflysu

 7. What is the value after executing the Python expression 10–3 *2+2.0?
    10–3*2+2.0 hk mhs;ka m%ldYkh l%shd;aul l< úg ,efnk w.h l=ula o?

    1) 16                  2) 16.0          3) 6                 4) 6.0              5) 28
                                                                                                     ( 2013 – 16 )
    Answer: 4)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    The correct answer is `(4) 6.0`. According to Python's order of operations (PEMDAS/BODMAS),
    multiplication is performed before addition and subtraction. So, `3 * 2` is calculated first, giving `6`. Then
    the expression becomes `10 - 6 + 2.0`. Next, subtraction and addition are performed from left to right: `10
    - 6` equals `4`, and `4 + 2.0` equals `6.0`. The result is a floating-point number because of the `2.0` in the
    expression. / ksjerÈ ms<s;=r jkafka (4) 6.0 fõ' mhs;ka ys fufyhqï wkqms<sfj,g (PEMDAS/BODMAS)
    wkqj" tl;= lsÍu iy wvq lsÍu isÿ lsÍug fmr .=K lsÍu isÿ flf¾' tneúka" `3 * 2` m<uqj .Kkh
    lr `6` ,nd fohs' túg m%ldYkh `10 - 6 + 2.0` njg m;afõ' B<Õg" wvq lsÍu iy tl;= lsÍu jfï
    isg ol=Kg isÿ flfra( `10 - 6` `4` g iudk jk w;r" `4 + 2.0` `6.0` g iudk fõ' m%ldYkfha we;s
    `2.0` ksid m%;sM,h bmsf,k ,laIH ixLHdjls'
    10 – 3 * 2 + 2.0 = ?
    10 – 6 + 2.0 = ?
    4 + 2.0 = ?
    4 + 2.0 = 6.0
    This evaluation order and result type behavior is the same in both Python 2 and Python 3

 8. Which of the following is a syntactically correct Python program?
    my; i|yka oE w;=frka ldrl Í;shdkql+,j ksjerÈ mhs;ka l%uf,aLkhla jkafka l=ula o?

             return a                            return a                     else return b
          else                                else:
             return b                            return b

          if(a > b):                          if(a > b):
             return a                            return a
          else:                               else:
             return b                            return b

    Answer: 2)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    The correct answer is (2). In Python, functions are defined using the `def` keyword, followed by the
    function name, parameters in parentheses, and a colon `:` to indicate the start of the function body.
    Statements within the function must be indented. Additionally, Python uses colons `:` to start conditional
    blocks (`if` and `else`).
    ksjerÈ ms<s;=r jkafka (2) fõ' Python ys" Y%s; w¾: oelafjkafka `def` uQ, moh Ndú;d lrñks" bkamiq

    `else`) wdrïN lsÍug `:` fld,ka Ndú;d lrhs'

    Why the others are incorrect ?
    wfkla tAjd jerÈ jkafka wehs ?

    (1) and (3) are missing colons `:` after `def` and `if`.
    (1) iy (3) ys `def` iy `if` g miq `:` fld,ka fhdod fkdue;'

    (3) incorrectly uses `then`, which is not valid in Python.
    (3) jerÈ f,i `then` Ndú;d lrhs" th Python ys j,x.= fkdfõ'

    (4) misses the colon `:` after the function definition (`def`).
    (4) Y%s; w¾: oelaùu (`def`) g miqj `:` ;Srej uÕ yßhs'

    (5) uses `function` instead of `def`, which is invalid in Python.
    (5) `def` fjkqjg `function` Ndú;d lrhs" th Python ys j,x.= fkdfõ'

    This syntax is consistent in both Python 2 and Python 3.
    fuu jdlH LKavh Python 2 iy Python 3 hk foflysu wkql+, fõ'

    Therefore, option 2) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 2) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

     value `0` is printed, and the algorithm stops.

    is never executed, and the program proceeds to terminate without any further output.

 ➢ Option / úl,amh 4)

   to read and write than binary machine code.

 2. Which of the followings is an invalid Python variable name?
    mhs;ka úp,H kdu i|yd j,x.= fkdjkafka my; i|yka ljrla o?

    1) MyCountry                     2) mycountry                     3) My country
    4) My_country                    5) _my_country_

                                                                                                 ( 2014 – 05 )
    Answer: 3)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    In Python, variable names must adhere to specific rules to be valid. They must begin with a letter (a-z, A-
    Z) or an underscore (_) and can only include alphanumeric characters (a-z, A-Z, 0-9) and underscores.
    Spaces are not allowed in variable names, and Python is case-sensitive, meaning `MyCountry` and
    `mycountry` would be treated as different variables. Reserved keywords cannot be used as variable names
    either. These rules ensure clarity and consistency in code.
    Python ys" úp,H kï j,x.= ùug ksYaÑ; kS;sj,g wkql+, úh hq;=h' tAjd (a-z, A-Z) wl=rlska fyda
    underscore (_) wl=rlska wdrïN úh hq;= w;r wlaIrdxl wlaIr (a-z, A-Z, 0-9) iy underscore muKla
    we;=<;a l< yelsh' úp,H kï j, ysia;eka ;eîug wjir ke;" iy Python hkq wlaIr-ixfõ§ fõ"
    tkï `MyCountry` iy `mycountry` úúO úp,H f,i i,lkq ,efí' fjka l< uQ, mo úp,H kï
    f,io Ndú;d l< fkdyel' fuu kS;s fla;fha meyeÈ,s nj iy wkql+,;dj iy;sl lrhs'

    For the given options, `MyCountry`, `mycountry`, `My_country` and `_my_country_` all follow Python's
    naming conventions and are valid variable names. However, `My country` is invalid because it contains a
    space, which is not allowed in variable names. This rule applies equally to both Python 2 and Python 3, as
    variable naming conventions have remained consistent across these versions.
    ,nd § we;s úl,am i|yd" `MyCountry`, `mycountry`, `my_country` iy `_my_country_` hk ish,a,u
    Python ys kï lsÍfï iïuq;Ska wkq.ukh lrk w;r j,x.= úp,H kï fõ' flfia fj;;a" `My
    country` j,x.= fkdjk neúka tys úp,H kï j, bvla wvx.= fõ' fuu Í;sh Python 2 iy Python
    3 hk folgu iudkj wod< fõ" ukao fuu wkqjdo mqrd úp,H kï lsÍfï iïuq;Ska ia:djrj mj;S'

    Therefore, option 3) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 3) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 3. Which of the following is a syntactically correct Python function?

                                                    return a
                                                  else
                                                    return b

    Answer: 2)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    In Python, a syntactically correct function must start with the `def` keyword, include valid parameters
    inside parentheses, and end the function header with a colon (`:`). The function body must be properly
    indented. Let’s analyze each option
    Python ys" ldrluh jYfhka ksjerÈ Y%s;hla kï def uQ, mofhka wdrïN úh hq;= w;r" jryka ;=<
    j,x.= mrdñ;Ska we;=<;a l< hq;= w;r" Y%s; YS¾Ih fld,khlska (`:`) wjika l< hq;=h' Y%s;fhys body
    fldgi ksis f,i indent l< hq;=h' wms iEu úl,amhlau úYaf,aIKh lruq

    Option / úl,amh 1)
    While this function appears correct at first glance, it lacks proper indentation for the `return` statement.
    Python requires the function body to be indented, so this option is invalid.
    fuu Y%s;h uq,skau neÆ ne,aug ksjerÈ nj fmkqko" thg return m%ldYh i|yd ksis indentation
    fkdue;' Python i|yd Y%s; body fldgi indent lsÍu wjYH fõ" tneúka fuu úl,amh j,x.= fkdfõ'

    Option / úl,amh 2)
    This function uses the `def` keyword, has a valid name (`isLarger`), includes parameters (`a, b`), ends the
    header with a colon (`:`), and the `return` statement is properly indented. This is a valid Python function.
    fuu Y%s;h def uQ, moh Ndú;d lrhs" j,x.= kula we; (`isLarger`)" mrdñ;Ska (`a, b`) we;=<;a fõ"
    YS¾Ih fld,khlska (`:`) wjika lrhs" iy `return` m%ldYh ksis f,i indentation lr we;' fuh
    j,x.= Python Y%s;hls'

    Option / úl,amh 3)
    This function is invalid because the function header does not end with a colon (`:`), which is a required
    syntax in Python.
    fuu Y%s;h j,x.= fkdjkafka Y%s; YS¾Ih fld,khlska (`:`) wjika fkdjk neúks" th Python ys wjYH
    ldrl Í;shls'

    Option / úl,amh 4)
    This function is invalid because it uses the `function` keyword, which is not used in Python for defining
    functions. Python uses the `def` keyword instead.
    fuu Y%s;h j,x.= fkdjkafka th Y%s; w¾: oelaùu i|yd Python ys Ndú;d fkdlrk function uQ,
    mohla Ndú;d lrk neúks' Python tA fjkqjg def uQ, moh Ndú;d lrhs'

 6. What will be the output when the following Python code is executed?
    my; olajd we;s mhs;ka fla;h l%shd;aul l< úg m%;sodkh l=ula o?

    a = ['a',2,[3,'b',4],[6,"abc",9],8]
    print(a[2][2])

    1) 2                2) [3, 'b', 4]      3) 'b'              4) 4                 5) 22

                                                                                                    ( 2014 – 40 )
    Answer: 4)
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    a is a list containing strings, integers and sublists. In Python, each element of a list has a specific index
    number. Index numbers are zero-based, meaning they start at 0 for the first element. The index numbers
    for the list `['a', 2, [3, 'b', 4], [6, "abc", 9], 8]` are,
    a hkq strings iy mQ¾K ixLHd hk o;a; j¾. j,ska iukaú; ,ehsia;=jls' fuu ,ehsia;=j ;=, Wm
    ,ehs;= ^sub list& o we;' mhs;ka ys ,ehsia;=jl iEu uQ,dx.hlgu ksYaÑ; wkql%ñl wxlhla we;'
    wkql%ñl ixLHd Y=kH ^0& u; mokï fõ" tkï tAjd m<uq uQ,dx.h i|yd 0 isg wdrïN fõ' `['a', 2, [3,
    'b', 4], [6, "abc", 9], 8]` ,ehsia;=j i|yd wkql%ñl ixLHd fufia fõ,

    First, the number of elements in the list `a` is 5. These elements are as follows,
    m<uqj" a ,ehsia;=fõ we;s uQ,dx. .Kk 5 ls' fuu uQ,dx. my; mßÈ fõ"
                                                                          Element            Index No
                                                                    1st          'a'             0

                                                                    4th    [6, "abc", 9]         3
                                                                    5th           8              4
    The 3rd and 4th elements are sublists. These sublists also have serial numbers for their elements, which
    are as follows,
    3 jk iy 4 jk uQ,dx. Wm ,ehsia;= fõ' fuu Wm ,ehsia;= j, uQ,dx. i|ydo wkql%ñl wxl we;"
    tAjd my; mßÈ fõ"

                                                                 3rd         4          2
                                                                     4th Element         Index No
                                                                    1st        6             0
                                                                    2nd      "abc"           1
                                                                    3rd        9             2

    Therefore, after executing the statement print(a[2][2]) as given in the code, the output is 4.
    tneúka" fla;fha olajd we;s mßÈ print(a[2][2]) m%ldYh l%shd;aul lsÍfuka miq" m%;sodkh jkafka 4
    fõ'
    Therefore, option 4) is the correct answer.

 7. What is the value of variable z after executing the Python statement z = 1 = = 2?
    z = 1 = = 2 hk mhs;ka j.ka;sh l%shd;aul l< miq úp,Hh i|yd ,efnk w.h l=ula o?

    1) 0                2) 1                 3) True                4) False            5) Null

                                                                                                       ( 2014 – 41 )
    Answer: 4)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    In the given Python statement `z = 1 == 2`, the expression `1 == 2` is evaluated first because it is a
    comparison operation. The comparison checks whether the value `1` is equal to `2`, which is not true.
    Therefore, the result of the comparison is `False`. This result is then assigned to the variable `z`. Hence,
    after executing the statement, `z` holds the boolean value `False`.
    § we;s mhs;ka m%ldYkfha `z = 1 == 2` ys" `1 == 2` m%ldYkh uq,skau we.hSug ,la lrkq ,nkafka th
    ixikaokd;aul fufyhqula jk neúks' ixikaokh uÕska `1` w.h `2` g iudko hkak mÍlaId lrhs"
    th i;H fkdfõ' tneúka" ixikaokfha m%;sM,h `False` fõ' bkamiq fuu m%;sM,h `z` úp,Hhg mjrkq
    ,efí' tneúka" m%ldYh l%shd;aul lsÍfuka miq" `z` ys nQ,shka w.h `False` fõ'

    This happens because in Python, the equality operator (`==`) evaluates to a boolean value, either `True`
    or `False`. Here, the expression `1 == 2` evaluates to `False` because the values being compared are not
    equal. The assignment operator (`=`) then stores this boolean value in the variable `z`. As a result, the final
    value of `z` is `False`.
    fuh isÿjkafka mhs;ka ys" ixikaokd;aul l%shdlre (`==`) `True` fyda `False` hk nQ,shka w.hlg
    we.hSug ,la lrk neúks' fuys§" `1 == 2` m%ldYkh `False` g we.hSug ,la lrkafka ixikaokh
    lrk w.hka iudk fkdjk neúks' mejreï l%shdlre (`=`) miqj fuu nQ,shka w.h `z` úp,Hfha .nvd
    lrhs' tys m%;sM,hla f,i" `z` ys wjidk w.h `False` fõ'

    Therefore, option 4) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 4) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 30. Which of the following Python expressions shows the correct evaluation order of the Python
     expression 10 - 4 * 3/2 - 5?
     10 - 4 * 3/2 - 5 hk mhs;ka m%ldYkfha we.hSfï ksjerÈ mámdáh my; i|yka l=ulska fmkakqï lrhso?

    1) (((10 - 4) * 3)/2) - 5           2) ((10 - (4 * 3))/2) - 5              3) 10 - (4 * ((3/2) - 5))
    4) 10 - ((4 * (3/2) - 5)            5) (10 - ((4 * 3)/2)) - 5

                                                                                                       ( 2014 – 42 )
    Answer: 5)
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    The correct evaluation order of the Python expression `10 - 4 * 3 / 2 - 5` is represented by the expression
    `(10 - ((4 * 3) / 2)) - 5`. This is because Python follows the operator precedence rules, where multiplication
    (`*`) and division (`/`) are evaluated before subtraction (`-`), and operations with the same precedence
    level are executed from left to right. Hence, `4 * 3` is evaluated first, followed by the division of the result
    by 2, then the subtraction of this intermediate result from 10, and finally subtracting 5 to yield the final
    value.
    10 - 4 * 3 / 2 - 5 hk mhs;ka m%ldYkfha ksjerÈ we.hSï wkqms<sfj, (10 - ((4 * 3) / 2)) - 5 hk m%ldYkh
    u.ska ksrEmKh flf¾' fuhg fya;=j mhs;ka l%shdlre mQ¾jdo¾Y Í;s wkq.ukh lrk neúks" tys§
    .=K lsÍu (`*`) iy fn§u (`/`) wvq lsÍu (`-`) g fmrwe.hSug ,la lrk w;r tlu mQ¾jdo¾Y
    uÜgula iys; fufyhqï jfï isg ol=Kg l%shd;aul fõ' tneúka" 4 * 3 m<uqj we.hSug ,la lrkq
    ,nk w;r" miqj m%;sM,h 2 ka fn§u" miqj fuu w;rueÈ m%;sM,h 10 ka wvq lsÍu iy wjidkfha
    5 la wvq lsÍfuka wjidk w.h ,nd .kS'

    Therefore, option 5) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 5) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 8. Consider the following statements about syntax or semantic errors in Python programming language:
    mhs;ka NdIdfõ ldrl Í;s fyda Yíod¾: fodaI fyda iïnkaOfhka my; i|yka j.ka;s i,ld n,kak(
    A. A program with syntax errors will not run to the end of the program.
       ldrl Í;s fodaI iys; l%uf,aLhla tys wjidkh olajd Odjkh fkdfõ'
    B. A program with only semantic errors will not run to its end.
       Yíod¾: fodaI muKla we;s l%uf,aLhla tys wjidkh olajd Odjkh fkdfõ'
    C. Syntax errors in programs are also called logical errors.
       l%uf,aL ldrl Í;sj, we;s fodaI ;d¾lsl fodaI f,i o ye¢kafõ'
    D. Programs with semantic errors may not produce correct outputs for some inputs.
       Yíod¾: fodaI iys; l%uf,aL iuyr wdodk i|yd ksjerÈ m%;sodk ,nd fkdÈh yels h'

    1) A and B only.    2) A and C only.    3) A and D only. 4) B and C only.        5) B and D only.
       A yd B muKs.        A yd C muKs.        A yd D muKs.     B yd C muKs.            B yd D muKs.

                                                                                                 ( 2014 – 50 )
    Answer: 3)
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    In Python programming, syntax errors occur when the code violates the rules of the language, such as
    missing colons, incorrect indentation, or misspelled keywords. Because syntax errors prevent the
    interpreter from understanding the code, a program containing them will not run to completion it will stop
    immediately when a syntax error is encountered. This means statement A is correct. On the other hand,
    semantic errors (or logical errors) happen when the code runs without crashing but produces incorrect
    results due to faulty logic or incorrect assumptions. Unlike syntax errors, semantic errors do not stop the
    program from running to its end, so statement B is false. Statement C is incorrect because syntax errors
    are not called logical errors; logical errors refer specifically to semantic errors, where the program runs
    but behaves incorrectly. Statement D is correct because programs with semantic errors may run fully but
    produce wrong or unexpected outputs depending on the inputs.
    mhs;ka l%uf,aLkfha§" ldrl fodaI we;s jkafka fla;h NdIdfõ kS;s W,a,x>kh lrk úgh" tkï
    fld,ka fkdue;s ùu" jerÈ bkafvkafÜIka fyda jerÈ wlaIr úkHdi iys; uQ, mo jeks' jdlH LKav
    fodaI mßj¾;lhdg fla;h f;areï .ekSfuka j<lajk neúka" tAjd wvx.= jevigykla iïmQ¾K lsÍug
    l%shd;aul fkdfõ' jdlH LKav fodaIhla we;s jQ úg th jydu k;r fõ' fuhska woyia lrkafka m%ldYh
    A ksjerÈ njhs' wfkla w;g" Yíod¾: fodaI ^fyda ;d¾lsl fodaI& isÿjkafka fla;h ì| jeàulska
    f;drj l%shd;aul jk kuq;a fodaI iys; ;¾lkh fyda jerÈ Wml,amk fya;=fjka jerÈ m%;sM, ksmojk
    úgh' ldrl fodaI fuka fkdj" Yíod¾: fodaI jevigyk tys wjidkh olajd l%shd;aul ùu kj;ajkafka
    ke;'" tneúka m%ldYh B wi;H fõ' m%ldYh C ksjerÈ fkdfõ'" ukao ldrl fodaI ;d¾lsl fodaI f,i
    ye¢kafjkafka ke;. ;d¾lsl fodaI úfYaIfhka Yíod¾: fodaI fj; fhduq fõ" tys§ jevigyk l%shd;aul
    jk kuq;a jerÈ f,i yeisf¾' m%ldYh D ksjerÈ jkafka Yíod¾: fodaI iys; jevigyka iïmQ¾Kfhkau
    l%shd;aul úh yels kuq;a wdodk u; mokïj jerÈ fyda wkfmalaIs; m%;sodkhka ksmoúh yels neúks'

    Both Python 2 and Python 3 handle syntax and semantic errors in the same fundamental way. Syntax errors
    are detected during the parsing phase before execution, so in both versions, a program with syntax errors
    will fail immediately and not run to completion. Semantic errors, meanwhile, depend on the program logic
    and input data, and neither Python 2 nor Python 3 prevents the program from running to the end if only
    semantic errors are present. Therefore, the behavior of how syntax and semantic errors affect program
    execution is consistent between Python 2 and Python 3.
    mhs;ka 2 iy mhs;ka 3 hk folu ldrl Í;s iy Yíod¾: fodaI tlu uQ,sl wdldrhlska yiqrejhs'
    l%shd;aul lsÍug fmr ú.%y lsÍfï wÈhf¾§ ldrl Í;s fodaI wkdjrKh fõ" tneúka wkqjdo foflysu"
    ldrl Í;s fodaI iys; jevigykla jydu wid¾:l jk w;r iïmQ¾K lsÍug l%shd;aul fkdfõ' fï
    w;r" Yíod¾: fodaI" jevigyka ;¾lkh iy wdodk o;a; u; r|d mj;sk w;r" Yíod¾: fodaI
    muKla ;sfí kï" mhs;ka 2 fyda mhs;ka 3 jevigyk wjidkh olajd l%shd;aul ùu j<lajkafka ke;'
    tneúka" ldrl Í;s iy Yíod¾: fodaI jevigyka l%shd;aul lsÍug n,mdk wdldrh ms<sn| yeisÍu
    mhs;ka 2 iy mhs;ka 3 w;r iudk fõ'

    Therefore, option 3) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 3) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

          wi;Hhs'

          tl,ia lsÍfï jevigykla mßj¾;khlska f;drj ;j;a tll l%shd fkdlrhs' tneúka" fuh wi;Hhls'

 3. The algorithm represented by the flowchart is considered as a poor algorithm because it does not

 4. The algorithm terminates

    4) by printing the value 4 when it is given the input values 2,5,4 one after the other.
       w.hka 2, 5, 4 tllg miq tlla f,i wdodkh l< miq w.h 4 uqøKh lsÍfuks'
    5) by printing the value 9 when it is given the input values 2,5,4 one after the other.

    Answer: 5)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    Option / úl,amh 1)
    This option is unclear, because input/s for Y is/are not mentioned. If 5 is entered to X, then 5 inputs for Y
    will be taken. When N = 5, it terminates.
    fuu úl,amh wmeyeÈ,s h" ukao Y i|yd wdodk$h i|yka lr fkdue;s neúks' X fj; 5 we;=<;a
    l<fyd;a" Y i|yd wdodk 5 la .kq ,efí' N = 5 jk úg" th wjika fõ'

    Option / úl,amh 2)
    Here, N is equal to 1. When 0 is entered to X, we cannot satisfy the N = X condition by incrementing the
    value of N. So, it becomes an infinite loop.
    fuys§" N, 1 g iudk fõ' X fj; 0 we;=<;a l< úg" N ys w.h jeä lsÍfuka wmg N = X fldkafoaish
    imqrd,sh fkdyel' tneúka" th wkka; Æmhla njg m;afõ'

    Option / úl,amh 3)

    l%shdj,sh wkqlrKh lr m%;sodkh ;SrKh lruq

    At first, 2 is entered as the value of X. (N = 1, T = 0)
    m<uqj" X ys w.h f,i 2 we;=<;a lr we;' (N = 1, T = 0)

    Y1 = 5 → T = T + Y1 = 0 + 5 = 5.
    So, the value of T becomes 5, when 5 is entered to Y.
    tneúka" 5, Y fj; we;=<;a l< úg T ys w.h 5 njg m;afõ'

    As N is not equal to X, another input for Y is taken.
    N, X g iudk fkdjk neúka" Y i|yd ;j;a wdodkhla .kq ,efí'

    Y2 = 4 → T = T + Y2 = 4 + 5 = 9
    Then, the value of T becomes 9, when 4 is entered to Y.
    túg" 4, Y fj; we;=<;a l< úg T ys w.h 9 njg m;afõ'

    So, T is printed as 9, not 5. So, this option is incorrect.
    tneúka" T uqøKh lrkq ,nkafka 5 f,i fkdj 9 f,i h' tneúka" fuu úl,amh jerÈ h'

    Option / úl,amh 4)

    Answer: 5)
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Option / úl,amh 1)
    Loop runs while n <= x, so it also runs when n == x. So, this is not identical to the flowchart. It does not
    stop exactly when n == x, it goes until n > x. So, it performs one extra iteration, when it should be
    terminated.
    Æmh n <= x jk úg l%shd;aul jk w;r" th n == x jk úgo l%shd;aul fõ' tneúka" fuh .e,Sï
    igykg iudk fkdfõ' th n == x jk úg k;r fkdfõ" th n > x jk f;la hhs' tneúka" th wjika
    l< hq;= úg th tla wu;r mqkrdj¾;khla isÿ lrhs'
    Option / úl,amh 2)
    Same problem as in option 1). The program runs until n > x, having an extra iteration. print(t) is placed
    incorrectly. It should be placed after the loop. This makes the algorithm prints sum in every iteration.
    úl,am 1) ys we;s .eg¿ju fõ' jevigyk n > x jk f;la l%shd;aul jk w;r" wu;r mqkrdj¾;khla
    we;' print(t) jerÈ f,i ;nd we;' th Æmhg miqj ;eìh hq;=h' fuh we,af.dß;uh iEu

    Statement / m%ldYh D)

    each element in the list to be processed sequentially. Since the code contains an iteration, this statement is
    incorrect.

 8. What is the return data type of the function named " f "?
    “f” kue;s Ys%;fha" m%;Hd.uk (return) o;a; m%rEmh jkafka l=ula o?

    1) Integer           2) Float            3) Boolean           4) Tuple             5) List

                                                                                                       ( 2015 - 46 )
    Answer: 4)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    In the provided Python code / imhd we;s Python fla;fha

    def f(b):
        n1, n2 = b[0], b[0]
        for m in b:
            if m > n1:
                n1 = m
            if m < n2:
                n2 = m
        return n1, n2

    The function `f` accepts a parameter `b`, which is expected to be a list. It initializes two variables, `n1`
    and `n2`, with the first element of the list `b`. Using a `for` loop, it iterates through the list to find the
    maximum (`n1`) and minimum (`n2`) values. Finally, the function returns both values as a pair.
    f Y%s;h b mrdñ;shla ms<s.kS" th ,ehsia;=jla jkq we;ehs wfmalaId flf¾' th b ,ehsia;=fõ m<uq
    wx.h iuÕ n1 iy n2 úp,H folla wdrïN lrhs' for Æmhla Ndú;d lrñka" Wmßu (`n1`) iy wju
    (`n2`) w.hka fidhd .ekSug th ,ehsia;=j yryd mqkrdj¾;kh fõ' wjidk jYfhka" Y%s;h hq.,hla
    f,i w.hka folu wdmiq ,nd fohs'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                   -44-                                              #ictfromabc

    In Python, when two or more values are returned from a function, they are combined into a single tuple.
    Here, `f` returns `(n1, n2)`, which is a tuple containing the maximum and minimum values.
    Python ys" Y%s;hlska w.hka folla fyda jeä .Kkla wdmiq ,nd ÿka úg" tAjd ;ks tuple tlla njg
    tAldnoaO fõ' fuys§" f (n1, n2) wdmiq ,nd fohs" th Wmßu iy wju w.hka wvx.= tuple tlls'

    identify the largest and smallest values in the list.

    values. The `print(f(temp))` statement calls the function with the list `temp` as the argument and displays
    the result. The final output is `(45, -2.0)`, where `45` is the largest value in the list and `-2.0` is the smallest
    value. The returned values are enclosed in parentheses because they are part of a tuple.

 10. Consider the following Python program:
     my; fmkajd we;s mhs;ka l%uf,aLh i,lkak(
    temp = [23,45,2,–2,0]
    print(temp[::2])
    What is the output of the above program?
    by; l%uf,aLfha m%;sodkh l=ula o?

    1) [23,45]          2) [–2,0]           3) [23,2,0]        4) [2,–2,0]         5) [23,45,2,–2,0]

                                                                                                   ( 2015 - 48 )
    Answer: 3)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    The provided Python program uses list slicing to extract specific elements from the list temp. The list temp
    is defined as [23, 45, 2, -2, 0]. The slicing operation temp[::2] starts from the beginning of the list and
    selects every second element by skipping one element in between. In this case, the elements at indices 0,
    2 and 4 are selected, which correspond to the values 23, 2 and 0, respectively. As a result, the output of
    the program is the list [23, 2, 0].
    imhd we;s mhs;ka jevigyk temp ,ehsia;=fjka ksYaÑ; uQ,dx. Wmqgd .ekSu i|yd ,ehsia;= slicing
    Ndú;d lrhs' ,ehsia;= temp [23, 45, 2, -2, 0] f,i w¾: olajd we;' temp[::2] slicing fufyhqu ,ehsia;=fõ
    wdrïNfha isg wdrïN jk w;r tla uQ,dx.hla uÕ yeÍfuka iEu fojk uQ,dx.hlau f;dard .kS' fuu
    wjia:dfõ§" 0, 2 iy 4 o¾Ylj, uQ,dx. f;dard .kq ,efí" tAjd ms<sfj,ska 23, 2 iy 0 w.hkag
    wkqrEm fõ' tys m%;sM,hla f,i" jevigyfka m%;sodkh [23, 2, 0] ,ehsia;=j fõ'

    This behavior is consistent in both Python 2 and Python 3, as the slicing syntax and functionality remain
    the same across these versions. The slicing operation produces a new list that contains the selected
    elements, and the output [23, 2, 0] will be identical regardless of whether the program is run in Python 2
    or Python 3.
    fuu wkqjdo yryd slicing lsÍfï jdlH LKavh iy l%shdldÍ;ajh tf,iu mj;sk neúka" Python 2 iy
    Python 3 hk foflysu fuu yeisÍu wkql+, fõ' slicing fufyhqu f;dard.;a uQ,dx. wvx.= kj
    ,ehsia;=jla ksmojk w;r" jevigyk Python 2 fyda Python 3 ys l%shd;aul fõo hkak wod< fkdfõ
    [23, 2, 0] m%;sodkh iudk jkq we;'

       Step        Index      Value      Included in Result?
      Step 1         0         23               Yes
      Step 2         1         45                No
      Step 3         2          2               Yes
      Step 4         3         -2                No
      Step 5         4          0               Yes

    Therefore, option 3) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 3) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

   1. Consider the following statements about compilers and interpreters used in programming languages:
      l%uf,aLk NdIdjkays Ndú; jk iïmdol yd w¾: úkHdil (compilers and interpreters) iïnkaOfhka
      my; oelafjk j.ka;s i,lkak(
          A. Compilers/interpreters are not required to execute a program written in assembly language.
             Assembly NdIdfjka ,shd we;s l%uf,aLhla l%shd;aul lsÍu i|yd iïmdol fyda w¾: úkHdil
             wjYH fkdfõ'
          B. Compilers are not essential to execute a program in machine code.
             hka;% fla;j,ska we;s l%uf,aLhla l%shd;aul lsÍu i|yd iïmdol w;HjYH fkdfõ'
          C. An executable program is translated into a source program by a compiler.
             l%shd;aul l< yels (executable) l%uf,aLhla iïmdolhla u.ska uQ, l%uf,aLhla (source program)
             njg mßj¾;kh lrkq ,nhs'
          Which of the above statements is/are correct?
          by; j.ka;s w;=frka ksjerÈ jkafka"

          1) A only                     2) B only                     3) C only                     4) A and B only 5) B and C only
             A muKs                        B muKs                        C muKs                        A yd B muKs     B yd C muKs

                                                                                                                                                         ( 2016 - 24 )
          Answer: 2)

          Explanation /meyeÈ,s lsÍu

          Statement / m%ldYh A)
          This statement is incorrect. Assembly language requires an assembler, which is a type of translator, to
          convert the assembly code into machine code for execution. While compilers or interpreters are not
          directly involved,a translation step is still necessary. Hence,assertion that no translation is required is false.
          fuu m%ldYh ksjerÈ fkdfõ' l%shd;aul lsÍu i|yd Assembly fla;h hka;% fla;hla njg mßj¾;kh
          lsÍu i|yd Assembly NdIdjg assembler lrkafkl= wjYH fõ" th mßj¾;l j¾.hls' iïmdolhska
          fyda mßj¾;lhska iDcqju iïnkaO fkdjQj;a" mßj¾;k mshjrla ;ju;a wjYH fõ' tneúka"
          mßj¾;khla wjYH fkdjk njg lrk m%ldYh wi;H fõ'

          Statement / m%ldYh B)
          This statement is correct. Machine code is already in a format that can be directly executed by the
          processor, without the need for a compiler. Compilers are used to convert high-level languages into
          machine code, but once in machine code form, a compiler is not needed for execution.
          fuu m%ldYh ksjerÈ fõ' hdka;%sl fla;h oekgu;a iïmdolfhl=f.a wjYH;djhlska f;drj ilikh
          uÕska iDcqju l%shd;aul l< yels wdlD;shlska mj;S' by< uÜgfï NdId hka;% fla;hla njg
          mßj¾;kh lsÍu i|yd iïmdol Ndú;d lrkq ,efí" kuq;a hka;% fla; wdldrfhka .;a miq" l%shd;aul
          lsÍu i|yd iïmdolhla wjYH fkdfõ'

          Statement / m%ldYh C)
          This statement is incorrect. The role of a compiler is to translate source code (written in a high-level
          programming language) into an executable program, not the other way around. This reversal of the
          compiler's function makes the statement false.
          fuu m%ldYh ksjerÈ fkdfõ' iïmdolfhl=f.a ld¾hNdrh jkafka ^by< uÜgfï l%uf,aLk NdIdjlska
          ,shd we;s& m%Nj fla;h l%shd;aul l< yels jevigykla njg mßj¾;kh lsÍuhs" wfkla w;g fkdfõ'

 2. Consider the following statements about flowcharts:
    .e,Sï igyka iïnkaOfhka my; j.ka;s i,ld n,kak(

       .e,Sï igykla hkq we,af.daß;uhl Ñ;%l ksrEmKhla fõ'

    Which of the above statements is/are correct?
    by; j.ka;s w;=frka ksjerÈ jkafka"

       A muKs              B muKs              C muKs              A yd B muKs     B yd C muKs

    Answer: 4)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    Statement / m%ldYh A)
    This statement is correct. Flowcharts use symbols such as ovals, rectangles, and arrows to visually depict
    the sequence of steps in an algorithm. This graphical representation helps in understanding the logic and
    structure of the algorithm.
    fuu m%ldYh ksjerÈ fõ' we,af.dß;uhl mshjr wkqms<sfj, oDYHuh jYfhka ksrEmKh lsÍu i|yd
    .e,Sï igyka ´j,dldr" iDcqfldaKdi% iy B;, jeks ixfla; Ndú;d lrhs' fuu Ñ;%l ksrEmKh
    we,af.dß;ufha ;¾lkh iy jHqyh f;areï .ekSug WmldÍ fõ'

    Statement / m%ldYh B)
    This statement is correct. A flowchart can have multiple end points depending on the logic of the algorithm.
    For instance, in a decision-based process, different conditions may lead to separate termination points,
    making it valid to have more than one ‘stop’ symbol.
    fuu m%ldYh ksjerÈ fõ' we,af.dß;ufha ;¾lkh wkqj .e,Sï igyklg nyq wka; ,laIH ;sìh
    yelsh' WodyrKhla f,i" ;SrK u; mokï jQ l%shdj,shl§" úúO ;;ajhka fjku wjika ,laIHj,g
    fya;= úh yels w;r" th ’stop’ ixfla; tllg jvd ;sîu j,x.= lrhs'

    Statement / m%ldYh C)
    This statement is incorrect. Algorithms can be represented in multiple ways, including pseudocode, plain
    text, and programming languages, in addition to flowcharts. Flowcharts are one of many tools for
    representing algorithms but not the only method.
    fuu m%ldYh ksjerÈ fkdfõ' .e,Sï igyka j,g wu;rj" we,af.dß;u nyq wdldrj,ska ksrEmKh l<

    lsÍu i|yd fndfyda fuj,ï j,ska tlls" kuq;a tlu l%uh fkdfõ'

 3. The algorithm represented by the following flowchart reads 5 numbers and prints the sum of them.
    my; .e,Sï igyk u.ska ksrEms; we,af.daß;uh" ixLHd 5la lshjd tajdfha ftlHh uqøKh lrkq ,efí'

                    Start

                      A

                   tot = 0

                                     No
                      B?                         Print tot

                          Yes

             Read a value for x

                 tot = tot + x                    Stop

                   i=i+1

    In order to execute the above flowchart correctly, A and B should be replaced by ……
    respectively. Which of the followings is suitable to fill the blank in the above statement?
    by; .e,Sï igyk ksjerÈj l%shd;aul ùu i|yd A iy B ms<sfj<ska …… u.ska
    m%;sia:dmkh l< hq;= h' by; jelsfha ysia;ek msrùu i|yd iqÿiq jkafka l=ula o?
    1) i = 0 and i ≤ 5                        2) i = 1 and i = 5                3) i = 0 and i > 5
    4) i = 1 and i ≤ 5                        5) i = 1 and i ≥ 5

                                                                                                     ( 2016 - 29 )
    Answer: 4)
    The flowchart's logic involves initializing a counter variable `i` to begin at 1 and then iterating through a
    loop five times while checking if the condition `i <= 5` holds true. This ensures that the program reads
    five values, adds them to the total (`tot`), and increments `i` in each iteration. The condition `i <= 5`
    ensures the loop terminates once `i` exceeds 5, maintaining proper logic and preventing infinite loops.
    This logic is standard in Python and works identically in both Python 2 and Python 3.
    .e,Sï igyfka ;¾lkhg `i` counter úp,Hhla 1 ka wdrïN lr mia j;djla Æmhla yryd
    mqkrdj¾;kh lsÍug we;=<;a jk w;r" `i <= 5` fldkafoaish i;H oehs mÍlaId lrhs' fuh jevigyk
    w.hka myla lshjk nj;a" tAjd total (`tot`) g tl;= lrk nj;a" iEu mqkrdj¾;khl§u `i` jeä
    lrk nj;a iy;sl lrhs' `i <= 5` fldkafoaish `i` yS w.h 5 blaujk úg Æmh wjika jk nj;a" ksis
    ;¾lkh mj;ajd f.k hk nj;a iy wkka; jdr .Kkla Æm l%shd;aul ùfuka j<lajk nj;a iy;sl
    lrhs' fuu ;¾lh Python ys iïu; jk w;r Python 2 iy Python 3 hk foflysu iudkj l%shd
    lrhs'
    Since the expressions `i = 1` and `i <= 5` are basic and consistent across all Python versions, there is no
    difference in how Python 2 and Python 3 interpret them. Both versions treat `i = 1` as an initialization and
    `i <= 5` as a valid comparison. The flowchart’s logic aligns with fundamental programming principles,
    making the answer common across Python versions. Therefore, the correct choices for `A` and `B` are `i
    = 1` and `i <= 5`, irrespective of the Python version.
    `i = 1` iy `i <= 5` hk m%ldYk ish¨u Python wkqjdo yryd uQ,sl iy wkql+, jk neúka" Python 2
    iy Python 3 tAjd w¾:l:kh lrk wdldrfhys fjkila fkdue;' wkqjdo folu `i = 1`
    wdrïNllrKhla f,i iy `i <= 5` j,x.= ixikaokhla f,i i,lhs' .e,Sï igyfka ;¾lkh uQ,sl

    tneúka" Python wkqjdoh l=ula jqj;a" `A` iy `B` i|yd ksjerÈ f;aÍï `i = 1` iy `i <= 5` fõ'

 5. Consider the following Python statement:
    my; i|yka mhs;ka j.ka;sh i,lkak(

    temp = [23,45,2,-2,0][:2:]

    What would be the value of the variable temp after executing the above statement?
    by; j.ka;sh l%shd;aul jQ miq temp kï jQ úp,Hfhys mj;sk w.h l=ula o?

    1) 23,45             2) [23,45]           3) 23,2              4) [23,2]            5) [23,2,0]

                                                                                                        ( 2016 - 31 )
    Answer: 2)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    Python Statement / mhs;ka m%ldYkh
    temp = [23, 45, 2, -2, 0][:2:]

    According to Python 2 / Python 2 j,g wkqj
    In Python 2, the syntax for slicing a list is the same as in the current version. The list `[23, 45, 2, -2, 0]` is
    sliced using `[:2:]`. This slicing takes elements starting from the beginning (`:`) up to (but not including)
    index `2` (`2:`). Therefore, only the first two elements `[23, 45]` are selected. The final result stored in
    `temp` is `[23, 45]`, which is a list.
    Python 2 ys" ,ehsia;=jla slicing lsÍu i|yd jk ldrl Í;sh jkafka oekg mj;sk mhs;kayS we;s
    wdldrhgu fõ' [23, 45, 2, -2, 0] ,ehsia;=j [:2:] Ndú;fhka slice lr we;' fuu slicing lsÍu wdrïNfha
    (`:`) isg ^kuq;a we;=<;a fkdlr& `2` (`2:`) o¾Ylh olajd uQ,dx. .kS' tneúka" [23, 45] m<uq uQ,dx.
    fol muKla f;dard .kq ,efí' `temp` ys .nvd lr we;s wjidk m%;sM,h [23, 45] jk w;r th
    ,ehsia;=jls'

    According to Current Python / j;auka Python j,g wkqj
    The slicing behavior remains identical in the current version of Python. The statement `[:2:]` extracts
    elements from the beginning up to index `2`, and `[23, 45]` is stored in `temp`. The result is still a list and
    remains `[23, 45]`.
    Python ys j;auka wkqjdofha slicing lsÍfï yeisÍu iudkj mj;S' [:2:] m%ldYh wdrïNfha isg `2`
    o¾Ylh olajd uQ,dx. Wmqgd .kakd w;r [23, 45] `temp` ys .nvd lr we;' m%;sM,h ;ju;a ,ehsia;=jla
    jk w;r [23, 45] f,i mj;S'

    Therefore, option 2) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 2) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 7. Consider the following Python program segment:
    my; mhs;ka l%uf,aL LKavh i,lkak(
    d1 = "(1,2,3)"
    d2 = (1,2,3)
    d3 = [1,2,(1,2)]
    What would be the types of variables dl, d2 and d3 respectively after the execution of the program
    segment?
    by; l%uf,aL nKavh l%shd;aul lsÍfuka miq d1, d2 iy d3 hk úp,Hhkaf.a m%rEmhka ms<sfj<ska
    l=ula fõ o?

    1) tuple, tuple, tuple             2) string, tuple, tuple            3) char, tuple, list
    4) string, tuple, list             5) tuple, tuple, list

                                                                                                       ( 2016 - 40 )
    Answer: 4)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    According to Python 2's syntax rules / Python 2 yS ldrl Í;s j,g wkqj
    `d1` is a string because it is enclosed in quotation marks (`"(1,2,3)"`). `d2` is a tuple because it is defined
    using parentheses without quotes. `d3` is a list because it is enclosed in square brackets (`[1,2,(1,2)]`), and
    the third element of the list is a tuple.
    `d1` hkq WoaOD; ,l=Kq (`"(1,2,3)"`) ;=< oud we;s neúka th string tlls' `d2` hkq WoaOD; ,l=Kq
    fkdue;sj jryka Ndú;fhka w¾: olajd we;s neúka th gm,a tlls' `d3` hkq y;/ia jryka
    (`[1,2,(1,2)]`) ;=< oud we;s neúka th ,ehsia;=jls" iy ,ehsia;=fõ ;=kajk uQ,dx.h gm,a tlls'

    According to the current Python's syntax rules / j;auka mhs;ka ldrl Í;s j,g wkqj
    The behavior is the same as in Python 2. `d1` is a string, `d2` is a tuple, and `d3` is a list containing a tuple
    as its third element.
    yeisÍu mhs;ka 2 ys fuka u fõ' `d1` hkq string tlls" `d2` hkq gm,a tlla jk w;r `d3` hkq tys
    ;=kajk uQ,dx.h f,i gm,a tlla wvx.= ,ehsia;=jls'

    Therefore, option 4) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 4) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 8. Which of the following Python statements is syntactically incorrect?
    my; i|yka l=uk mhs;ka j.kaksh ldrl Í;s wkqj jerÈ jkafka o?
    1) a, b = 10, 15                         2) a = b = 1, 2                  3) a = 1, 2
    4) a, b = 2, (3, 5)                      5) a, b = 2, 3, 5

                                                                                                  ( 2016 - 41 )
    Answer: 5)

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    According to Python 2's syntax rules / Python 2 yS ldrl Í;s j,g wkqj
    Option (5) is syntactically incorrect because the number of variables on the left-hand side (a, b) does not
    match the number of values on the right-hand side (2, 3, 5). In Python, unpacking requires the number of
    variables to equal the number of values. The other options are valid because they adhere to Python's rules
    for tuple assignment and unpacking.
    jï mi we;s úp,H .Kk (a, b) ol=Kq mi we;s w.hka .Kk (2, 3, 5) iuÕ fkd.e<fmk neúka
    úl,amh (5) ldrl Í;s j,g wkQj ksjerÈ fkdfõ' Python ys" weiqreï bj;a lsÍu i|yd w.hka
    .Kkg iudk úp,H .Kk wjYH fõ' wfkla úl,am j,x.= jkafka tAjd gm,a mejreu iy m%idrKh
    i|yd mhs;kaf.a kS;sj,g wkql+, jk neúks'

    According to the current Python's syntax rules / j;auka mhs;ka ldrl Í;s j,g wkqj
    The behavior remains the same. Option (5) is invalid due to a mismatch in the number of variables and
    values, which causes a `ValueError`. The other options follow correct syntax, as tuple unpacking is handled
    the same way in both Python 2 and the current version.
    yeisÍu tf,iu mj;S' úp,H iy w.hka .Kfkys fkd.e<mSu fya;=fjka úl,amh (5) j,x.= fkdjk
    w;r th `ValueError` we;s lrhs' wfkl=;a úl,am ksjerÈ jdlH LKavh wkq.ukh lrhs" ukao gm,a
    bj;a lsÍu Python 2 iy j;auka wkqjdoh hk foflysu tlu wdldrhlska yiqrejkq ,efí'

    Therefore, option 5) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 5) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'
